========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it wasn't really my idea to come in. I work down at the fabrication plant—I run the heavy presses mostly. It's a tough job, really loud, and you have to wear these industrial earplugs that always give me a headache by Tuesday. My supervisor, Dave, he's a real stickler for the rules. He actually used to work over at the old auto plant before it shut down, so he thinks he's seen it all. 

Anyway, he made me go see occupational health. And they're the ones who sent me here. They said my weight is... well, they claim it's dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. But I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, you know? Just being more disciplined. 

And you really need discipline on the floor. It's kind of like when I was taking some classes at the community college a while back. If you weren't disciplined with your schedule, you'd just fall behind immediately. I had this one professor for a business ethics class who wouldn't even let you in the door if you were a minute late. He'd just lock it right at 8:00 AM. But yeah, occupational health wouldn't sign off on my clearance until I came to see you.

Afraid? I mean... yeah. Honestly, if the scale goes up even a little bit, I just completely panic. I get so scared that I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and just end up heavy again.

Fat? I mean... yeah, definitely. When I see myself in the reflection of the shop windows, or even just in the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look too big. And honestly, once I see that, it completely ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. 

It's actually a lot like the mirrors we have set up at the blind corners in the warehouse. You know, those big convex ones? They put them up after this forklift accident last year. One of the drivers—I think his name was Gary, older guy, always wore those bright orange suspenders—he came around the corner too fast and clipped a pallet of steel coils. It was a huge mess. The coils unspooled everywhere, and it took maintenance like two days to clean it up. They had to bring in this special heavy-duty winch just to move the metal out of the aisle. Gary didn't even get fired, which is crazy, but they did make him take this awful three-day safety course. I heard the instructor for that course used to be a state trooper, so he was super intense about everything... wait, sorry, what was the question again?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... I'm not really sure. I don't know if I can even separate the two, honestly. If I lose control, I'm going to look awful, so it's just all tangled up together in my head. I don't really know which one is the main thing. 

But losing control... that is a terrifying feeling. It’s sort of like when the automated lathe we use for the high-density plastics just completely went haywire last month. The programming got corrupted or something, and it started carving these deep, jagged grooves into the stock instead of shaving it down. Hot plastic chips were flying everywhere, hitting the safety glass like hail. The emergency stop button actually got jammed, so Mark, our floor manager, had to sprint over and rip the main electrical breaker down. Mark's been so stressed out lately, though. His wife just filed for divorce, and he’s been living out of this old Winnebago he parked behind the diner off Route 9. I feel terrible for the guy, but he started bringing his dog to the shop because he can't leave it in the RV, and this dog—it’s a pug, I think, or maybe a bulldog mix—it just sneezes constantly. Like, all over the breakroom floor. And nobody wants to say anything to him because of the divorce, but someone is going to slip on it eventually. I almost did when I was getting my thermos out of the fridge on Tuesday... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about control?

Health? I mean... no, not really. I don't really think about the health part of it at all. It's just about the weight, you know? Keeping it down. If I'm thin, I'm fine. That's all that matters.

It's the only thing that really counts. \n\nIt makes putting on my work clothes so stressful every morning. Actually, speaking of the work clothes, we have this industrial laundry service that brings our heavy canvas coveralls every Monday. The driver, Hector, he always parks his massive delivery truck right in front of the main loading dock, which drives the receiving guys absolutely insane. Last week he left the engine idling while he hauled the uniform bins inside, and someone had propped the breakroom door open to get a breeze, so the whole room just filled up with diesel exhaust. We were all sitting in there choking on fumes. Brenda was trying to eat this really strong-smelling tuna salad sandwich at the same time, and between the fish smell and the diesel, it was just a nightmare. Brenda's always bringing in these elaborate lunches, though. She has this insulated cooler bag that actually looks exactly like a designer purse, I think her daughter bought it for her for Mother's Day... wait, sorry, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it makes you look or about feeling like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I don't know if I can even separate the two, honestly. If I lose control, I'm going to look awful, so it's just all tangled up together in my head. I don't really know which one is the main thing. \n\nBut losing control... that is a terrifying feeling. It’s sort of like when the automated lathe we use for the high-density plastics just completely went haywire last month. The programming got corrupted or something, and it started carving these deep, jagged grooves into the stock instead of shaving it down. Hot plastic chips were flying everywhere, hitting the safety glass like hail. The emergency stop button actually got jammed, so Mark, our floor manager, had to sprint over and rip the main electrical breaker down. Mark's been so stressed out lately, though. His wife just filed for divorce, and he’s been living out of this old Winnebago he parked behind the diner off Route 9. I feel terrible for the guy, but he started bringing his dog to the shop because he can't leave it in the RV, and this dog—it’s a pug, I think, or maybe a bulldog mix—it just sneezes constantly. Like, all over the breakroom floor. And nobody wants to say anything to him because of the divorce, but someone is going to slip on it eventually. I almost did when I was getting my thermos out of the fridge on Tuesday... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about control?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that gaining weight could affect your health?\n\nPatient: Health? I mean... no, not really. I don't really think about the health part of it at all. It's just about the weight, you know? Keeping it down. If I'm thin, I'm fine. That's all that matters. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you mention health, though, because they just made us all do this mandatory CPR and first aid training at the shop. The company brought in this guy, Dave, who used to be a paramedic. He had this dummy that we all had to practice chest compressions on, but the dummy was so old the chest plate kept making this loud clicking noise, like a broken metronome. Dave said he's had that dummy since 1998. He named it Resusci-Randy. Anyway, during the training, Sarah—she's the one who runs the CNC router—she got way too into it and actually snapped one of the plastic ribs inside the dummy. Dave was so mad, he went totally red in the face. It turns out Dave is really serious about his equipment because he runs this side business selling vintage medical supplies at flea markets. He was telling me he just found an antique glass syringe in its original velvet-lined box at an estate sale last weekend, and he thinks he can get like two hundred bucks for it from this collector in Ohio... wait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my health?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you worry about being unable to stop eating once you start?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I don't really have that problem, honestly. I don't start eating, so I don't have to worry about stopping. It's just easier to avoid it altogether. If I don't take that first bite, I'm safe.

Yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I have to, right? Most days I just stick to coffee before my early shift, and then maybe half of a protein bar at lunch. I don't know... I guess? I mean, I can't really imagine not being worried about gaining weight. It's just always there, you know? Like this constant noise in the back of my head. So I don't really know what I'd do if it wasn't there.

He actually used to work over at the old auto plant before it shut down, so he thinks he's seen it all. \n\nAnyway, he made me go see occupational health. And they're the ones who sent me here. They said my weight is... well, they claim it's dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. But I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, you know? Just being more disciplined. \n\nAnd you really need discipline on the floor. It's kind of like when I was taking some classes at the community college a while back. If you weren't disciplined with your schedule, you'd just fall behind immediately. I had this one professor for a business ethics class who wouldn't even let you in the door if you were a minute late. He'd just lock it right at 8:00 AM. But yeah, occupational health wouldn't sign off on my clearance until I came to see you.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid? I mean... yeah. Honestly, if the scale goes up even a little bit, I just completely panic. I get so scared that I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and just end up heavy again. \n\nIt’s a lot like... well, it's like when the calibration is off on the stamping press at work. If the pressure is off by even a fraction, the whole batch of sheet metal gets ruined. We had this massive order for custom brackets a few weeks ago, and this new guy, Tommy—he just started, sweet kid but doesn't pay attention—he didn't check the gauges right. We had to scrap like two entire pallets of aluminum. Dave was furious. He made us all stay three hours late on a Friday to recalibrate everything and redo the run. And my car's AC was broken that day, so sitting in rush hour traffic on the interstate going home was just miserable. I ended up taking it to this mechanic my brother recommended, but I swear they overcharged me for the freon... wait, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Fat? I mean... yeah, definitely. When I see myself in the reflection of the shop windows, or even just in the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look too big. And honestly, once I see that, it completely ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the mirrors we have set up at the blind corners in the warehouse. You know, those big convex ones? They put them up after this forklift accident last year. One of the drivers—I think his name was Gary, older guy, always wore those bright orange suspenders—he came around the corner too fast and clipped a pallet of steel coils. It was a huge mess. The coils unspooled everywhere, and it took maintenance like two days to clean it up. They had to bring in this special heavy-duty winch just to move the metal out of the aisle. Gary didn't even get fired, which is crazy, but they did make him take this awful three-day safety course. I heard the instructor for that course used to be a state trooper, so he was super intense about everything... wait, sorry, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, pretty much. Like I was just saying about the shop windows and the bathroom mirror... if my stomach or my thighs look too big to me, that's it. It ruins how I feel about myself entirely. It doesn't matter if I did my hair or whatever. If the weight isn't right, I just feel completely worthless and ugly. It's the only thing that really counts. \n\nIt makes putting on my work clothes so stressful every morning. Actually, speaking of the work clothes, we have this industrial laundry service that brings our heavy canvas coveralls every Monday. The driver, Hector, he always parks his massive delivery truck right in front of the main loading dock, which drives the receiving guys absolutely insane. Last week he left the engine idling while he hauled the uniform bins inside, and someone had propped the breakroom door open to get a breeze, so the whole room just filled up with diesel exhaust. We were all sitting in there choking on fumes. Brenda was trying to eat this really strong-smelling tuna salad sandwich at the same time, and between the fish smell and the diesel, it was just a nightmare. Brenda's always bringing in these elaborate lunches, though. She has this insulated cooler bag that actually looks exactly like a designer purse, I think her daughter bought it for her for Mother's Day... wait, sorry, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it makes you look or about feeling like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I don't know if I can even separate the two, honestly. If I lose control, I'm going to look awful, so it's just all tangled up together in my head. I don't really know which one is the main thing. \n\nBut losing control... that is a terrifying feeling. It’s sort of like when the automated lathe we use for the high-density plastics just completely went haywire last month. The programming got corrupted or something, and it started carving these deep, jagged grooves into the stock instead of shaving it down. Hot plastic chips were flying everywhere, hitting the safety glass like hail. The emergency stop button actually got jammed, so Mark, our floor manager, had to sprint over and rip the main electrical breaker down. Mark's been so stressed out lately, though. His wife just filed for divorce, and he’s been living out of this old Winnebago he parked behind the diner off Route 9. I feel terrible for the guy, but he started bringing his dog to the shop because he can't leave it in the RV, and this dog—it’s a pug, I think, or maybe a bulldog mix—it just sneezes constantly. Like, all over the breakroom floor. And nobody wants to say anything to him because of the divorce, but someone is going to slip on it eventually. I almost did when I was getting my thermos out of the fridge on Tuesday... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about control?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that gaining weight could affect your health?\n\nPatient: Health? I mean... no, not really. I don't really think about the health part of it at all. It's just about the weight, you know? Keeping it down. If I'm thin, I'm fine. That's all that matters. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you mention health, though, because they just made us all do this mandatory CPR and first aid training at the shop. The company brought in this guy, Dave, who used to be a paramedic. He had this dummy that we all had to practice chest compressions on, but the dummy was so old the chest plate kept making this loud clicking noise, like a broken metronome. Dave said he's had that dummy since 1998. He named it Resusci-Randy. Anyway, during the training, Sarah—she's the one who runs the CNC router—she got way too into it and actually snapped one of the plastic ribs inside the dummy. Dave was so mad, he went totally red in the face. It turns out Dave is really serious about his equipment because he runs this side business selling vintage medical supplies at flea markets. He was telling me he just found an antique glass syringe in its original velvet-lined box at an estate sale last weekend, and he thinks he can get like two hundred bucks for it from this collector in Ohio... wait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my health?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being unable to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: I don't really have that problem, honestly. I don't start eating, so I don't have to worry about stopping. It's just easier to avoid it altogether. If I don't take that first bite, I'm safe. \n\nSpeaking of not eating, though, our vending machine in the breakroom has been completely broken for like three weeks now. The coil on C4—the one that holds the peanut butter crackers—got jammed, and now it just spins and spins without dropping anything. This guy from maintenance, Luis, tried to fix it by shaking the whole machine, and he ended up knocking a bunch of the candy bars off the top shelf into this weird gap behind the glass. Now they're just sitting there, melting because the cooling unit is busted too. Luis is usually pretty good at fixing things, but he's been super distracted lately because he's trying to build a deck on the back of his house by himself. He showed me pictures on his phone, and he’s using this composite decking material that looks like real wood but apparently doesn't splinter. He said he got a huge discount on it because the lumber yard ordered the wrong color for some big contractor, so it was just sitting in their warehouse taking up space... wait, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Something about eating?\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types of food you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I have to, right? Most days I just stick to coffee before my early shift, and then maybe half of a protein bar at lunch. Then I'll have a small bowl of vegetables after my yoga class. If I eat more than that, or if I eat different things, the whole day just feels completely out of control and I panic about the weight. \n\nIt's actually really hard to even find those protein bars I like anymore. The grocery store near my house stopped carrying the vanilla almond ones. I asked the manager about it—this guy named Greg who always wears these really loud, floral Hawaiian shirts even in the middle of winter—and he said their distributor changed their minimum order quantities or something. Greg is a nice guy, but he talks really loud. Like, he doesn't have an inside voice at all. He was telling me all about his son's marching band competition while we were standing in the cereal aisle, and everybody was staring at us. His son plays the tuba, which I guess is really heavy, and they had to travel all the way to Scranton for this regional championship. They took this huge charter bus, but the air conditioning on the bus broke, and it was unseasonably warm that weekend, so all these high school kids in heavy wool band uniforms were just sweating through their clothes for four hours on the highway. Greg said they had to stop at a rest stop and buy out the entire vending machine's supply of Gatorade just to keep the kids from passing out... wait, sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask me about my food?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess? I mean, I can't really imagine not being worried about gaining weight. It's just always there, you know? Like this constant noise in the back of my head. So I don't really know what I'd do if it wasn't there. \n\nSpeaking of noise in the back of your head, though, the CNC machine I was running yesterday was making this horrible high-pitched whining sound all afternoon. It was driving me absolutely crazy. I had to wear my earplugs and my over-ear muffs just to tolerate it. I told my supervisor, Mark, about it, and he said it's probably the spindle bearings going bad again. We just replaced them like six months ago, but Mark thinks the night shift guys are running the feed rates way too high and burning them out. Mark gets so frustrated with the night shift. He actually started keeping a logbook in his office, writing down every little thing they do wrong. It's a leather-bound book, looks like something out of a pirate movie. He got it at this Renaissance Faire he goes to every year with his wife. They dress up and everything. He was showing me pictures of his costume—he goes as this medieval blacksmith, complete with the leather apron and these heavy iron tongs he forged himself. He said it gets incredibly hot walking around in all that leather in July, but he drinks this stuff they call \"mead,\" which is basically honey wine, I think? He said he drank too much of it last year and fell asleep under a hay wagon... wait, what was I saying? Did you ask me about my weight again?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It was driving me absolutely crazy. I had to wear my earplugs and my over-ear muffs just to tolerate it. I told my supervisor, Mark, about it, and he said it's probably the spindle bearings going bad again. He actually started keeping a logbook in his office, writing down every little thing they do wrong. It's a leather-bound book, looks like something out of a pirate movie. He got it at this Renaissance Faire he goes to every year with his wife. They dress up and everything. He was showing me pictures of his costume—he goes as this medieval blacksmith, complete with the leather apron and these heavy iron tongs he forged himself. He said he drank too much of it last year and fell asleep under a hay wagon... wait, what was I saying? Did you ask me about my weight again?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... occupational health at the plant actually sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low. They were making a huge deal about it, saying it's not safe for someone running the heavy presses and doing metal fabrication all day. But I honestly just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined with myself. 

But occupational health is always overreacting about something. Last month, they made this huge fuss because they thought the ventilation in the welding bay wasn't up to code. They brought in this guy with all these meters and sensors—he looked like a Ghostbuster. His name was Dave, I think. Dave had this really bad comb-over, like he was trying to hide a bald spot but the wind kept catching it anyway. He spent three days walking around the shop floor holding this little wand up in the air. He told me he used to do environmental testing for the military, out in the desert somewhere in Nevada. He had all these crazy stories about testing soil samples near old nuclear sites. He said the sand out there turns into glass when it gets hot enough, which I guess makes sense if you think about how glass is made. I actually took a glassblowing class once at the community college before I dropped out. It was fascinating, but the furnace they use—they call it a glory hole, which always made everyone laugh—was so incredibly hot. You have to keep turning the pipe constantly or the glass just drips onto the floor like honey. The instructor was this woman who made these beautiful, delicate glass flowers, but she had burn scars all up and down her forearms from... wait, I'm sorry. I lost track. Were you asking about my weight again?

I heard the instructor for that course used to be a state trooper, so he was super intense about everything... wait, sorry, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, pretty much. Like I was just saying about the shop windows and the bathroom mirror... if my stomach or my thighs look too big to me, that's it. It ruins how I feel about myself entirely. It doesn't matter if I did my hair or whatever. If the weight isn't right, I just feel completely worthless and ugly. It's the only thing that really counts. \n\nIt makes putting on my work clothes so stressful every morning. Actually, speaking of the work clothes, we have this industrial laundry service that brings our heavy canvas coveralls every Monday. The driver, Hector, he always parks his massive delivery truck right in front of the main loading dock, which drives the receiving guys absolutely insane. Last week he left the engine idling while he hauled the uniform bins inside, and someone had propped the breakroom door open to get a breeze, so the whole room just filled up with diesel exhaust. We were all sitting in there choking on fumes. Brenda was trying to eat this really strong-smelling tuna salad sandwich at the same time, and between the fish smell and the diesel, it was just a nightmare. Brenda's always bringing in these elaborate lunches, though. She has this insulated cooler bag that actually looks exactly like a designer purse, I think her daughter bought it for her for Mother's Day... wait, sorry, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it makes you look or about feeling like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I don't know if I can even separate the two, honestly. If I lose control, I'm going to look awful, so it's just all tangled up together in my head. I don't really know which one is the main thing. \n\nBut losing control... that is a terrifying feeling. It’s sort of like when the automated lathe we use for the high-density plastics just completely went haywire last month. The programming got corrupted or something, and it started carving these deep, jagged grooves into the stock instead of shaving it down. Hot plastic chips were flying everywhere, hitting the safety glass like hail. The emergency stop button actually got jammed, so Mark, our floor manager, had to sprint over and rip the main electrical breaker down. Mark's been so stressed out lately, though. His wife just filed for divorce, and he’s been living out of this old Winnebago he parked behind the diner off Route 9. I feel terrible for the guy, but he started bringing his dog to the shop because he can't leave it in the RV, and this dog—it’s a pug, I think, or maybe a bulldog mix—it just sneezes constantly. Like, all over the breakroom floor. And nobody wants to say anything to him because of the divorce, but someone is going to slip on it eventually. I almost did when I was getting my thermos out of the fridge on Tuesday... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about control?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that gaining weight could affect your health?\n\nPatient: Health? I mean... no, not really. I don't really think about the health part of it at all. It's just about the weight, you know? Keeping it down. If I'm thin, I'm fine. That's all that matters. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you mention health, though, because they just made us all do this mandatory CPR and first aid training at the shop. The company brought in this guy, Dave, who used to be a paramedic. He had this dummy that we all had to practice chest compressions on, but the dummy was so old the chest plate kept making this loud clicking noise, like a broken metronome. Dave said he's had that dummy since 1998. He named it Resusci-Randy. Anyway, during the training, Sarah—she's the one who runs the CNC router—she got way too into it and actually snapped one of the plastic ribs inside the dummy. Dave was so mad, he went totally red in the face. It turns out Dave is really serious about his equipment because he runs this side business selling vintage medical supplies at flea markets. He was telling me he just found an antique glass syringe in its original velvet-lined box at an estate sale last weekend, and he thinks he can get like two hundred bucks for it from this collector in Ohio... wait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my health?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being unable to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: I don't really have that problem, honestly. I don't start eating, so I don't have to worry about stopping. It's just easier to avoid it altogether. If I don't take that first bite, I'm safe. \n\nSpeaking of not eating, though, our vending machine in the breakroom has been completely broken for like three weeks now. The coil on C4—the one that holds the peanut butter crackers—got jammed, and now it just spins and spins without dropping anything. This guy from maintenance, Luis, tried to fix it by shaking the whole machine, and he ended up knocking a bunch of the candy bars off the top shelf into this weird gap behind the glass. Now they're just sitting there, melting because the cooling unit is busted too. Luis is usually pretty good at fixing things, but he's been super distracted lately because he's trying to build a deck on the back of his house by himself. He showed me pictures on his phone, and he’s using this composite decking material that looks like real wood but apparently doesn't splinter. He said he got a huge discount on it because the lumber yard ordered the wrong color for some big contractor, so it was just sitting in their warehouse taking up space... wait, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Something about eating?\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types of food you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I have to, right? Most days I just stick to coffee before my early shift, and then maybe half of a protein bar at lunch. Then I'll have a small bowl of vegetables after my yoga class. If I eat more than that, or if I eat different things, the whole day just feels completely out of control and I panic about the weight. \n\nIt's actually really hard to even find those protein bars I like anymore. The grocery store near my house stopped carrying the vanilla almond ones. I asked the manager about it—this guy named Greg who always wears these really loud, floral Hawaiian shirts even in the middle of winter—and he said their distributor changed their minimum order quantities or something. Greg is a nice guy, but he talks really loud. Like, he doesn't have an inside voice at all. He was telling me all about his son's marching band competition while we were standing in the cereal aisle, and everybody was staring at us. His son plays the tuba, which I guess is really heavy, and they had to travel all the way to Scranton for this regional championship. They took this huge charter bus, but the air conditioning on the bus broke, and it was unseasonably warm that weekend, so all these high school kids in heavy wool band uniforms were just sweating through their clothes for four hours on the highway. Greg said they had to stop at a rest stop and buy out the entire vending machine's supply of Gatorade just to keep the kids from passing out... wait, sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask me about my food?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess? I mean, I can't really imagine not being worried about gaining weight. It's just always there, you know? Like this constant noise in the back of my head. So I don't really know what I'd do if it wasn't there. \n\nSpeaking of noise in the back of your head, though, the CNC machine I was running yesterday was making this horrible high-pitched whining sound all afternoon. It was driving me absolutely crazy. I had to wear my earplugs and my over-ear muffs just to tolerate it. I told my supervisor, Mark, about it, and he said it's probably the spindle bearings going bad again. We just replaced them like six months ago, but Mark thinks the night shift guys are running the feed rates way too high and burning them out. Mark gets so frustrated with the night shift. He actually started keeping a logbook in his office, writing down every little thing they do wrong. It's a leather-bound book, looks like something out of a pirate movie. He got it at this Renaissance Faire he goes to every year with his wife. They dress up and everything. He was showing me pictures of his costume—he goes as this medieval blacksmith, complete with the leather apron and these heavy iron tongs he forged himself. He said it gets incredibly hot walking around in all that leather in July, but he drinks this stuff they call \"mead,\" which is basically honey wine, I think? He said he drank too much of it last year and fell asleep under a hay wagon... wait, what was I saying? Did you ask me about my weight again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... occupational health at the plant actually sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low. They were making a huge deal about it, saying it's not safe for someone running the heavy presses and doing metal fabrication all day. But I honestly just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined with myself. \n\nBut occupational health is always overreacting about something. He said the sand out there turns into glass when it gets hot enough, which I guess makes sense if you think about how glass is made. I actually took a glassblowing class once at the community college before I dropped out. It was fascinating, but the furnace they use—they call it a glory hole, which always made everyone laugh—was so incredibly hot. You have to keep turning the pipe constantly or the glass just drips onto the floor like honey. The instructor was this woman who made these beautiful, delicate glass flowers, but she had burn scars all up and down her forearms from... wait, I'm sorry. I lost track. Yeah, probably. I mean, even with everyone at the plant telling me I'm too small, whenever I catch my reflection in the shop window or the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look so big to me, and seeing that honestly just ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the entire day. 

The bathroom mirrors at the plant are terrible anyway, though. The fluorescent lights above them have been flickering for like three weeks straight. It gives you a migraine if you stare too long. I put in a work order with Stan, the maintenance guy, but he's been totally tied up trying to keep the old yellow forklift running. That thing is a total dinosaur. The whole back half of the shop smells like burnt oil and pine needles now. It actually kind of reminds me of my grandfather's garage. He used to make these incredibly intricate wooden birdhouses. He’d sit out there for hours with a little hand saw, cutting out these tiny little cedar shingles for the roofs. He even painted them to match the actual houses of the people he gave them to. He gave one to my Aunt Betty, who lived in this bright pink Victorian house, so he painted the birdhouse bright pink with white trim, and he even put a little copper weather vane on top that actually spun when the wind blew. The birds never actually nested in it, though, because Betty had these three huge outdoor cats that would just sit on the porch and stare at it all day... wait, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? They said my weight is... well, they claim it's dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. But I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, you know? Just being more disciplined. \n\nAnd you really need discipline on the floor. It's kind of like when I was taking some classes at the community college a while back. If you weren't disciplined with your schedule, you'd just fall behind immediately. I had this one professor for a business ethics class who wouldn't even let you in the door if you were a minute late. He'd just lock it right at 8:00 AM. But yeah, occupational health wouldn't sign off on my clearance until I came to see you.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid? I mean... yeah. Honestly, if the scale goes up even a little bit, I just completely panic. I get so scared that I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and just end up heavy again. \n\nIt’s a lot like... well, it's like when the calibration is off on the stamping press at work. If the pressure is off by even a fraction, the whole batch of sheet metal gets ruined. We had this massive order for custom brackets a few weeks ago, and this new guy, Tommy—he just started, sweet kid but doesn't pay attention—he didn't check the gauges right. We had to scrap like two entire pallets of aluminum. Dave was furious. He made us all stay three hours late on a Friday to recalibrate everything and redo the run. And my car's AC was broken that day, so sitting in rush hour traffic on the interstate going home was just miserable. I ended up taking it to this mechanic my brother recommended, but I swear they overcharged me for the freon... wait, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Fat? I mean... yeah, definitely. When I see myself in the reflection of the shop windows, or even just in the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look too big. And honestly, once I see that, it completely ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the mirrors we have set up at the blind corners in the warehouse. You know, those big convex ones? They put them up after this forklift accident last year. One of the drivers—I think his name was Gary, older guy, always wore those bright orange suspenders—he came around the corner too fast and clipped a pallet of steel coils. It was a huge mess. The coils unspooled everywhere, and it took maintenance like two days to clean it up. They had to bring in this special heavy-duty winch just to move the metal out of the aisle. Gary didn't even get fired, which is crazy, but they did make him take this awful three-day safety course. I heard the instructor for that course used to be a state trooper, so he was super intense about everything... wait, sorry, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, pretty much. Like I was just saying about the shop windows and the bathroom mirror... if my stomach or my thighs look too big to me, that's it. It ruins how I feel about myself entirely. It doesn't matter if I did my hair or whatever. If the weight isn't right, I just feel completely worthless and ugly. It's the only thing that really counts. \n\nIt makes putting on my work clothes so stressful every morning. Actually, speaking of the work clothes, we have this industrial laundry service that brings our heavy canvas coveralls every Monday. The driver, Hector, he always parks his massive delivery truck right in front of the main loading dock, which drives the receiving guys absolutely insane. Last week he left the engine idling while he hauled the uniform bins inside, and someone had propped the breakroom door open to get a breeze, so the whole room just filled up with diesel exhaust. We were all sitting in there choking on fumes. Brenda was trying to eat this really strong-smelling tuna salad sandwich at the same time, and between the fish smell and the diesel, it was just a nightmare. Brenda's always bringing in these elaborate lunches, though. She has this insulated cooler bag that actually looks exactly like a designer purse, I think her daughter bought it for her for Mother's Day... wait, sorry, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it makes you look or about feeling like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I don't know if I can even separate the two, honestly. If I lose control, I'm going to look awful, so it's just all tangled up together in my head. I don't really know which one is the main thing. \n\nBut losing control... that is a terrifying feeling. It’s sort of like when the automated lathe we use for the high-density plastics just completely went haywire last month. The programming got corrupted or something, and it started carving these deep, jagged grooves into the stock instead of shaving it down. Hot plastic chips were flying everywhere, hitting the safety glass like hail. The emergency stop button actually got jammed, so Mark, our floor manager, had to sprint over and rip the main electrical breaker down. Mark's been so stressed out lately, though. His wife just filed for divorce, and he’s been living out of this old Winnebago he parked behind the diner off Route 9. I feel terrible for the guy, but he started bringing his dog to the shop because he can't leave it in the RV, and this dog—it’s a pug, I think, or maybe a bulldog mix—it just sneezes constantly. Like, all over the breakroom floor. And nobody wants to say anything to him because of the divorce, but someone is going to slip on it eventually. I almost did when I was getting my thermos out of the fridge on Tuesday... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about control?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that gaining weight could affect your health?\n\nPatient: Health? I mean... no, not really. I don't really think about the health part of it at all. It's just about the weight, you know? Keeping it down. If I'm thin, I'm fine. That's all that matters. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you mention health, though, because they just made us all do this mandatory CPR and first aid training at the shop. The company brought in this guy, Dave, who used to be a paramedic. He had this dummy that we all had to practice chest compressions on, but the dummy was so old the chest plate kept making this loud clicking noise, like a broken metronome. Dave said he's had that dummy since 1998. He named it Resusci-Randy. Anyway, during the training, Sarah—she's the one who runs the CNC router—she got way too into it and actually snapped one of the plastic ribs inside the dummy. Dave was so mad, he went totally red in the face. It turns out Dave is really serious about his equipment because he runs this side business selling vintage medical supplies at flea markets. He was telling me he just found an antique glass syringe in its original velvet-lined box at an estate sale last weekend, and he thinks he can get like two hundred bucks for it from this collector in Ohio... wait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my health?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being unable to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: I don't really have that problem, honestly. I don't start eating, so I don't have to worry about stopping. It's just easier to avoid it altogether. If I don't take that first bite, I'm safe. \n\nSpeaking of not eating, though, our vending machine in the breakroom has been completely broken for like three weeks now. The coil on C4—the one that holds the peanut butter crackers—got jammed, and now it just spins and spins without dropping anything. This guy from maintenance, Luis, tried to fix it by shaking the whole machine, and he ended up knocking a bunch of the candy bars off the top shelf into this weird gap behind the glass. Now they're just sitting there, melting because the cooling unit is busted too. Luis is usually pretty good at fixing things, but he's been super distracted lately because he's trying to build a deck on the back of his house by himself. He showed me pictures on his phone, and he’s using this composite decking material that looks like real wood but apparently doesn't splinter. He said he got a huge discount on it because the lumber yard ordered the wrong color for some big contractor, so it was just sitting in their warehouse taking up space... wait, I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Something about eating?\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types of food you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I have to, right? Most days I just stick to coffee before my early shift, and then maybe half of a protein bar at lunch. Then I'll have a small bowl of vegetables after my yoga class. If I eat more than that, or if I eat different things, the whole day just feels completely out of control and I panic about the weight. \n\nIt's actually really hard to even find those protein bars I like anymore. The grocery store near my house stopped carrying the vanilla almond ones. I asked the manager about it—this guy named Greg who always wears these really loud, floral Hawaiian shirts even in the middle of winter—and he said their distributor changed their minimum order quantities or something. Greg is a nice guy, but he talks really loud. Like, he doesn't have an inside voice at all. He was telling me all about his son's marching band competition while we were standing in the cereal aisle, and everybody was staring at us. His son plays the tuba, which I guess is really heavy, and they had to travel all the way to Scranton for this regional championship. They took this huge charter bus, but the air conditioning on the bus broke, and it was unseasonably warm that weekend, so all these high school kids in heavy wool band uniforms were just sweating through their clothes for four hours on the highway. Greg said they had to stop at a rest stop and buy out the entire vending machine's supply of Gatorade just to keep the kids from passing out... wait, sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask me about my food?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess? I mean, I can't really imagine not being worried about gaining weight. It's just always there, you know? Like this constant noise in the back of my head. So I don't really know what I'd do if it wasn't there. \n\nSpeaking of noise in the back of your head, though, the CNC machine I was running yesterday was making this horrible high-pitched whining sound all afternoon. It was driving me absolutely crazy. I had to wear my earplugs and my over-ear muffs just to tolerate it. I told my supervisor, Mark, about it, and he said it's probably the spindle bearings going bad again. We just replaced them like six months ago, but Mark thinks the night shift guys are running the feed rates way too high and burning them out. Mark gets so frustrated with the night shift. He actually started keeping a logbook in his office, writing down every little thing they do wrong. It's a leather-bound book, looks like something out of a pirate movie. He got it at this Renaissance Faire he goes to every year with his wife. They dress up and everything. He was showing me pictures of his costume—he goes as this medieval blacksmith, complete with the leather apron and these heavy iron tongs he forged himself. He said it gets incredibly hot walking around in all that leather in July, but he drinks this stuff they call \"mead,\" which is basically honey wine, I think? He said he drank too much of it last year and fell asleep under a hay wagon... wait, what was I saying? Did you ask me about my weight again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... occupational health at the plant actually sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low. They were making a huge deal about it, saying it's not safe for someone running the heavy presses and doing metal fabrication all day. But I honestly just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined with myself. \n\nBut occupational health is always overreacting about something. Last month, they made this huge fuss because they thought the ventilation in the welding bay wasn't up to code. They brought in this guy with all these meters and sensors—he looked like a Ghostbuster. His name was Dave, I think. Dave had this really bad comb-over, like he was trying to hide a bald spot but the wind kept catching it anyway. He spent three days walking around the shop floor holding this little wand up in the air. He told me he used to do environmental testing for the military, out in the desert somewhere in Nevada. He had all these crazy stories about testing soil samples near old nuclear sites. He said the sand out there turns into glass when it gets hot enough, which I guess makes sense if you think about how glass is made. I actually took a glassblowing class once at the community college before I dropped out. It was fascinating, but the furnace they use—they call it a glory hole, which always made everyone laugh—was so incredibly hot. You have to keep turning the pipe constantly or the glass just drips onto the floor like honey. The instructor was this woman who made these beautiful, delicate glass flowers, but she had burn scars all up and down her forearms from... wait, I'm sorry. I lost track. Were you asking about my weight again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if other people thought you were underweight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, even with everyone at the plant telling me I'm too small, whenever I catch my reflection in the shop window or the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look so big to me, and seeing that honestly just ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the entire day. \n\nThe bathroom mirrors at the plant are terrible anyway, though. The fluorescent lights above them have been flickering for like three weeks straight. It gives you a migraine if you stare too long. I put in a work order with Stan, the maintenance guy, but he's been totally tied up trying to keep the old yellow forklift running. That thing is a total dinosaur. It’s always leaking hydraulic fluid all over the loading dock, and it creates this massive slip hazard. They have to dump bags and bags of this cheap pine sawdust over the puddles to soak it up. The whole back half of the shop smells like burnt oil and pine needles now. It actually kind of reminds me of my grandfather's garage. He used to make these incredibly intricate wooden birdhouses. He’d sit out there for hours with a little hand saw, cutting out these tiny little cedar shingles for the roofs. He even painted them to match the actual houses of the people he gave them to. He gave one to my Aunt Betty, who lived in this bright pink Victorian house, so he painted the birdhouse bright pink with white trim, and he even put a little copper weather vane on top that actually spun when the wind blew. The birds never actually nested in it, though, because Betty had these three huge outdoor cats that would just sit on the porch and stare at it all day... wait, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What did you ask me?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I think I already said this, but it's really the control thing. Like, if the scale goes up even a little bit, I just panic. I get so scared that I'm going to lose all the control I've worked so hard for and end up heavy again. I just can't let that happen.

I know I keep saying it's about control, but... I guess it really is about how I look, too. I know I just said this, but I really don't think so. I mean, my family keeps saying I look sick, and the shop nurse checked my vitals the other day and acted like it was some huge emergency. But I keep telling them, I'm still working double shifts, I'm still running every morning, and I'm getting everything done. So it can't be that serious, right? 

The shop nurse, though, her name is Patty, and she is just always overreacting to everything. Last week, one of the guys from shipping got a tiny papercut from a cardboard box, and she insisted on wrapping his whole finger in this giant, bulky bandage like he'd severed an artery. She has this huge supply closet full of bandages in every shape and size. I saw inside it once when the door was propped open, and she had these really cool neon-colored bandages. They were bright pink and green, like the kind they put on kids when they get their shots at the pediatrician. 

My niece just had to get a bunch of shots for kindergarten, and she threw an absolute fit. My sister had to bribe her with a trip to the ice cream shop afterward. They went to that place on Main Street, the one that makes their own waffle cones. You can smell the waffle cones baking from like three blocks away. It smells amazing, like vanilla and toasted sugar. They have this one flavor called "Moose Tracks" that has little peanut butter cups mixed into vanilla ice cream, but the peanut butter cups are so hard from being frozen that you almost crack a tooth when you bite into them. I don't understand why people like stuff with huge chunks in it anyway, it just ruins the texture of the ice cream. Like, if I want a candy bar, I'll just eat a candy bar, you know? Why mix it into ice cream and make it impossible to chew... wait. Sorry. What was the question again? Was it about Patty?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it wasn't really my idea to come in. I work down at the fabrication plant—I run the heavy presses mostly. It's a tough job, really loud, and you have to wear these industrial earplugs that always give me a headache by Tuesday. My supervisor, Dave, he's a real stickler for the rules. He actually used to work over at the old auto plant before it shut down, so he thinks he's seen it all. \n\nAnyway, he made me go see occupational health. And they're the ones who sent me here. They said my weight is... well, they claim it's dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. But I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, you know? Just being more disciplined. \n\nAnd you really need discipline on the floor. It's kind of like when I was taking some classes at the community college a while back. If you weren't disciplined with your schedule, you'd just fall behind immediately. I had this one professor for a business ethics class who wouldn't even let you in the door if you were a minute late. He'd just lock it right at 8:00 AM. But yeah, occupational health wouldn't sign off on my clearance until I came to see you.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid? I mean... yeah. Honestly, if the scale goes up even a little bit, I just completely panic. I get so scared that I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and just end up heavy again. \n\nIt’s a lot like... well, it's like when the calibration is off on the stamping press at work. If the pressure is off by even a fraction, the whole batch of sheet metal gets ruined. We had this massive order for custom brackets a few weeks ago, and this new guy, Tommy—he just started, sweet kid but doesn't pay attention—he didn't check the gauges right. We had to scrap like two entire pallets of aluminum. Dave was furious. He made us all stay three hours late on a Friday to recalibrate everything and redo the run. And my car's AC was broken that day, so sitting in rush hour traffic on the interstate going home was just miserable. I ended up taking it to this mechanic my brother recommended, but I swear they overcharged me for the freon... wait, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Fat? I mean... yeah, definitely. When I see myself in the reflection of the shop windows, or even just in the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look too big. And honestly, once I see that, it completely ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the mirrors we have set up at the blind corners in the warehouse. You know, those big convex ones? They put them up after this forklift accident last year. One of the drivers—I think his name was Gary, older guy, always wore those bright orange suspenders—he came around the corner too fast and clipped a pallet of steel coils. It was a huge mess. The coils unspooled everywhere, and it took maintenance like two days to clean it up. They had to bring in this special heavy-duty winch just to move the metal out of the aisle. Gary didn't even get fired, which is crazy, but they did make him take this awful three-day safety course. I heard the instructor for that course used to be a state trooper, so he was super intense about everything... wait, sorry, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, pretty much. Like I was just saying about the shop windows and the bathroom mirror... if my stomach or my thighs look too big to me, that's it. It ruins how I feel about myself entirely. It doesn't matter if I did my hair or whatever. If the weight isn't right, I just feel completely worthless and ugly. It's the only thing that really counts. \n\nIt makes putting on my work clothes so stressful every morning. Actually, speaking of the work clothes, we have this industrial laundry service that brings our heavy canvas coveralls every Monday. The driver, Hector, he always parks his massive delivery truck right in front of the main loading dock, which drives the receiving guys absolutely insane. Last week he left the engine idling while he hauled the uniform bins inside, and someone had propped the breakroom door open to get a breeze, so the whole room just filled up with diesel exhaust. We were all sitting in there choking on fumes. What were we talking about? Something about eating?\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types of food you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. I mean, I have to, right? Most days I just stick to coffee before my early shift, and then maybe half of a protein bar at lunch. Then I'll have a small bowl of vegetables after my yoga class. If I eat more than that, or if I eat different things, the whole day just feels completely out of control and I panic about the weight. \n\nIt's actually really hard to even find those protein bars I like anymore. The grocery store near my house stopped carrying the vanilla almond ones. I asked the manager about it—this guy named Greg who always wears these really loud, floral Hawaiian shirts even in the middle of winter—and he said their distributor changed their minimum order quantities or something. Greg is a nice guy, but he talks really loud. Like, he doesn't have an inside voice at all. He was telling me all about his son's marching band competition while we were standing in the cereal aisle, and everybody was staring at us. His son plays the tuba, which I guess is really heavy, and they had to travel all the way to Scranton for this regional championship. They took this huge charter bus, but the air conditioning on the bus broke, and it was unseasonably warm that weekend, so all these high school kids in heavy wool band uniforms were just sweating through their clothes for four hours on the highway. Greg said they had to stop at a rest stop and buy out the entire vending machine's supply of Gatorade just to keep the kids from passing out... wait, sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask me about my food?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if you weren't worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess? I mean, I can't really imagine not being worried about gaining weight. It's just always there, you know? Like this constant noise in the back of my head. So I don't really know what I'd do if it wasn't there. \n\nSpeaking of noise in the back of your head, though, the CNC machine I was running yesterday was making this horrible high-pitched whining sound all afternoon. It was driving me absolutely crazy. I had to wear my earplugs and my over-ear muffs just to tolerate it. I told my supervisor, Mark, about it, and he said it's probably the spindle bearings going bad again. We just replaced them like six months ago, but Mark thinks the night shift guys are running the feed rates way too high and burning them out. Mark gets so frustrated with the night shift. He actually started keeping a logbook in his office, writing down every little thing they do wrong. It's a leather-bound book, looks like something out of a pirate movie. He got it at this Renaissance Faire he goes to every year with his wife. They dress up and everything. He was showing me pictures of his costume—he goes as this medieval blacksmith, complete with the leather apron and these heavy iron tongs he forged himself. He said it gets incredibly hot walking around in all that leather in July, but he drinks this stuff they call \"mead,\" which is basically honey wine, I think? He said he drank too much of it last year and fell asleep under a hay wagon... wait, what was I saying? Did you ask me about my weight again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what most people would consider normal for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... occupational health at the plant actually sent me here because they said my weight is dangerously low. They were making a huge deal about it, saying it's not safe for someone running the heavy presses and doing metal fabrication all day. But I honestly just thought I was getting leaner, you know? More disciplined with myself. \n\nBut occupational health is always overreacting about something. Last month, they made this huge fuss because they thought the ventilation in the welding bay wasn't up to code. They brought in this guy with all these meters and sensors—he looked like a Ghostbuster. His name was Dave, I think. Dave had this really bad comb-over, like he was trying to hide a bald spot but the wind kept catching it anyway. He spent three days walking around the shop floor holding this little wand up in the air. He told me he used to do environmental testing for the military, out in the desert somewhere in Nevada. He had all these crazy stories about testing soil samples near old nuclear sites. He said the sand out there turns into glass when it gets hot enough, which I guess makes sense if you think about how glass is made. I actually took a glassblowing class once at the community college before I dropped out. It was fascinating, but the furnace they use—they call it a glory hole, which always made everyone laugh—was so incredibly hot. You have to keep turning the pipe constantly or the glass just drips onto the floor like honey. It’s always leaking hydraulic fluid all over the loading dock, and it creates this massive slip hazard. They have to dump bags and bags of this cheap pine sawdust over the puddles to soak it up. He’d sit out there for hours with a little hand saw, cutting out these tiny little cedar shingles for the roofs. He even painted them to match the actual houses of the people he gave them to. He gave one to my Aunt Betty, who lived in this bright pink Victorian house, so he painted the birdhouse bright pink with white trim, and he even put a little copper weather vane on top that actually spun when the wind blew. Unattractive? No, I don't really care about that, honestly. It's not about how I look. It's more about... well, if the scale goes up even a little, I just panic that I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked for and end up heavy again.

He actually used to work over at the old auto plant before it shut down, so he thinks he's seen it all. \n\nAnyway, he made me go see occupational health. And they're the ones who sent me here. They said my weight is... well, they claim it's dangerously low for someone running presses and doing fabrication all day. But I honestly thought I was just getting leaner, you know? Just being more disciplined. \n\nAnd you really need discipline on the floor. It's kind of like when I was taking some classes at the community college a while back. If you weren't disciplined with your schedule, you'd just fall behind immediately. I had this one professor for a business ethics class who wouldn't even let you in the door if you were a minute late. He'd just lock it right at 8:00 AM. But yeah, occupational health wouldn't sign off on my clearance until I came to see you.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Afraid? I mean... yeah. Honestly, if the scale goes up even a little bit, I just completely panic. I get so scared that I’m going to lose all the control I’ve worked so hard for and just end up heavy again. \n\nIt’s a lot like... well, it's like when the calibration is off on the stamping press at work. If the pressure is off by even a fraction, the whole batch of sheet metal gets ruined. We had this massive order for custom brackets a few weeks ago, and this new guy, Tommy—he just started, sweet kid but doesn't pay attention—he didn't check the gauges right. We had to scrap like two entire pallets of aluminum. Dave was furious. He made us all stay three hours late on a Friday to recalibrate everything and redo the run. And my car's AC was broken that day, so sitting in rush hour traffic on the interstate going home was just miserable. I ended up taking it to this mechanic my brother recommended, but I swear they overcharged me for the freon... wait, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Fat? I mean... yeah, definitely. When I see myself in the reflection of the shop windows, or even just in the bathroom mirror at work, all I can focus on is my stomach and my thighs. They just look too big. And honestly, once I see that, it completely ruins how I feel about myself for the rest of the day. \n\nIt's actually a lot like the mirrors we have set up at the blind corners in the warehouse. You know, those big convex ones? They put them up after this forklift accident last year. One of the drivers—I think his name was Gary, older guy, always wore those bright orange suspenders—he came around the corner too fast and clipped a pallet of steel coils. It was a huge mess. The coils unspooled everywhere, and it took maintenance like two days to clean it up. They had to bring in this special heavy-duty winch just to move the metal out of the aisle. Gary didn't even get fired, which is crazy, but they did make him take this awful three-day safety course. I heard the instructor for that course used to be a state trooper, so he was super intense about everything... wait, sorry, what was the question again?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that determines whether you are attractive or not?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, pretty much. Like I was just saying about the shop windows and the bathroom mirror... if my stomach or my thighs look too big to me, that's it. It ruins how I feel about myself entirely. It doesn't matter if I did my hair or whatever. If the weight isn't right, I just feel completely worthless and ugly. It's the only thing that really counts. \n\nIt makes putting on my work clothes so stressful every morning. Actually, speaking of the work clothes, we have this industrial laundry service that brings our heavy canvas coveralls every Monday. The driver, Hector, he always parks his massive delivery truck right in front of the main loading dock, which drives the receiving guys absolutely insane. Last week he left the engine idling while he hauled the uniform bins inside, and someone had propped the breakroom door open to get a breeze, so the whole room just filled up with diesel exhaust. We were all sitting in there choking on fumes. Brenda was trying to eat this really strong-smelling tuna salad sandwich at the same time, and between the fish smell and the diesel, it was just a nightmare. Brenda's always bringing in these elaborate lunches, though. She has this insulated cooler bag that actually looks exactly like a designer purse, I think her daughter bought it for her for Mother's Day... wait, sorry, what were we talking about?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about how it makes you look or about feeling like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I don't know if I can even separate the two, honestly. If I lose control, I'm going to look awful, so it's just all tangled up together in my head. I don't really know which one is the main thing. \n\nBut losing control... that is a terrifying feeling. It’s sort of like when the automated lathe we use for the high-density plastics just completely went haywire last month. The programming got corrupted or something, and it started carving these deep, jagged grooves into the stock instead of shaving it down. Hot plastic chips were flying everywhere, hitting the safety glass like hail. The emergency stop button actually got jammed, so Mark, our floor manager, had to sprint over and rip the main electrical breaker down. Mark's been so stressed out lately, though. His wife just filed for divorce, and he’s been living out of this old Winnebago he parked behind the diner off Route 9. I feel terrible for the guy, but he started bringing his dog to the shop because he can't leave it in the RV, and this dog—it’s a pug, I think, or maybe a bulldog mix—it just sneezes constantly. Like, all over the breakroom floor. And nobody wants to say anything to him because of the divorce, but someone is going to slip on it eventually. I almost did when I was getting my thermos out of the fridge on Tuesday... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about control?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that gaining weight could affect your health?\n\nPatient: Health? I mean... no, not really. I don't really think about the health part of it at all. It's just about the weight, you know? Keeping it down. If I'm thin, I'm fine. That's all that matters. \n\nIt's actually kind of funny you mention health, though, because they just made us all do this mandatory CPR and first aid training at the shop. The company brought in this guy, Dave, who used to be a paramedic. He had this dummy that we all had to practice chest compressions on, but the dummy was so old the chest plate kept making this loud clicking noise, like a broken metronome. Dave said he's had that dummy since 1998. He named it Resusci-Randy. Anyway, during the training, Sarah—she's the one who runs the CNC router—she got way too into it and actually snapped one of the plastic ribs inside the dummy. Dave was so mad, he went totally red in the face. It turns out Dave is really serious about his equipment because he runs this side business selling vintage medical supplies at flea markets. He was telling me he just found an antique glass syringe in its original velvet-lined box at an estate sale last weekend, and he thinks he can get like two hundred bucks for it from this collector in Ohio... wait, sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my health?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being unable to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: I don't really have that problem, honestly. I don't start eating, so I don't have to worry about stopping. It's just easier to avoid it altogether. Something about my health?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about being unable to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: I don't really have that problem, honestly. I don't start eating, so I don't have to worry about stopping. It's just easier to avoid it altogether. If I don't take that first bite, I'm safe. \n\nSpeaking of not eating, though, our vending machine in the breakroom has been completely broken for like three weeks now. The coil on C4—the one that holds the peanut butter crackers—got jammed, and now it just spins and spins without dropping anything. This guy from maintenance, Luis, tried to fix it by shaking the whole machine, and he ended up knocking a bunch of the candy bars off the top shelf into this weird gap behind the glass.
